arXiv:2206.05900v1 [cs.LG] 13 Jun 2022

Provable Benefit of Multitask Representation Learning in Reinforcement Learning

Yuan Cheng ^†^†thanks: School of Statistics and Finance, University of Science and Technology of
China, Hefei 230026, China; e-mail: cy16@mail.ustc.edu.cn    Songtao Feng ^†^†thanks: School of
Electrical Engineering and Computer Science, The Pennsylvania State University, University Park, PA
16802, USA; e-mail: sxf302@psu.edu    Jing Yang ^†^†thanks: School of Electrical Engineering and
Computer Science, The Pennsylvania State University, University Park, PA 16802, USA; e-mail: 
yangjing@psu.edu    Hong Zhang ^†^†thanks: School of Statistics and Finance, University of Science
and Technology of China, Hefei 230026, China; e-mail: zhangh@mail.ustc.edu.cn    Yingbin Liang ^†^†
thanks: Department of Electrical and Computer Engineering, The Ohio State University, OH 43210,
USA; e-mail: liang.889@osu.edu

Abstract

As representation learning becomes a powerful technique to reduce sample complexity in
reinforcement learning (RL) in practice, theoretical understanding of its advantage is still
limited. In this paper, we theoretically characterize the benefit of representation learning under
the low-rank Markov decision process (MDP) model. We first study multitask low-rank RL (as upstream
training), where all tasks share a common representation, and propose a new multitask reward-free
algorithm called REFUEL. REFUEL learns both the transition kernel and the near-optimal policy for
each task, and outputs a well-learned representation for downstream tasks. Our result demonstrates
that multitask representation learning is provably more sample-efficient than learning each task
individually, as long as the total number of tasks is above a certain threshold. We then study the
downstream RL in both online and offline settings, where the agent is assigned with a new task
sharing the same representation as the upstream tasks. For both online and offline settings, we
develop a sample-efficient algorithm, and show that it finds a near-optimal policy with the
suboptimality gap bounded by the sum of the estimation error of the learned representation in
upstream and a vanishing term as the number of downstream samples becomes large. Our downstream
results of online and offline RL further capture the benefit of employing the learned
representation from upstream as opposed to learning the representation of the low-rank model
directly. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first theoretical study that characterizes the
benefit of representation learning in exploration-based reward-free multitask RL for both upstream
and downstream tasks.

1 Introduction

Multitask representation learning has arisen as a popular and powerful framework to deal with
data-scarce environment in supervised learning, bandits and reinforcement learning (RL) recently (
Maurer et al.,, 2016; Du et al.,, 2020; Tripuraneni et al.,, 2021; Yang et al.,, 2021; Cella
et al.,, 2022; Lu et al.,, 2021; Arora et al.,, 2020; Hu et al.,, 2021). The general multitask
representation learning framework consists of both upstream multiple source tasks learning and
downstream target task learning. In RL applications, in upstream, the agent is assigned with TT
source tasks sharing a common representation and the goal is to learn a near-optimal policy for
each task via learning the representation. In downstream, with the help of the learned
representation, the agent aims to find a near-optimal policy of a new task that shares the same
representation as the source tasks. While representation learning has achieved great success in
supervised learning (Du et al.,, 2020; Tripuraneni et al.,, 2021; Maurer et al.,, 2016; Kong
et al.,, 2020) and multi-armed bandits (MAB) problems (Yang et al.,, 2021; Qin et al.,, 2022; Cella
et al.,, 2022), most works in multitask RL mainly focus on empirical algorithms (Sodhani et al.,,
2021; Arulkumaran et al.,, 2022; Teh et al.,, 2017) with limited theoretical works (Arora et al.,,
2020; Hu et al.,, 2021; Brunskill and Li,, 2013; Müller and Pacchiano,, 2022; Calandriello et al.,,
2015; Lu et al.,, 2021; D’Eramo et al.,, 2020). Generally speaking, there are two main challenges
for multitask representation learning in RL. First, differently from supervised learning with
static datasets, in MAB and RL, representation learning and data collection are intertwined with
each other. The agent has to choose an exploration policy in each iteration based on the
representation learned in the past. Such iterative process introduces temporal dependence to the
collected data. Note that none of the aforementioned theoretical multitask RL studies took the
influence of sequential exploration and temporal dependence in data into consideration. Second,
data distribution mismatch between the target task and the source tasks may occur. Additional
assumption is usually required in order to ensure the representation learned from sources task will
benefit the target task (Lu et al.,, 2021).

In this work, we aim to unveil the fundamental impact of multitask representation learning on RL by
answering the following two open questions:

  • •
   
    First, in the upstream, is it possible to design exploration-based multitask representation
    learning algorithms for RL to achieve efficiency gain compared with single-task RL after taking
    the interactive data collection process and temporal data dependency into account?

  • •
   
    Second, can the learned representation from upstream bring efficiency gain in sample complexity
    to the downstream learning?

Main contributions: We focus on multitask representation learning under low-rank MDPs (Agarwal
et al.,, 2020), where the transition kernel admits a low-rank decomposition that naturally involves
representation learning. It answers the aforementioned questions affirmatively, as summarized
below.

  • •
   
    First, we propose a new multitask representation learning algorithm called REFUEL under
    low-rank MDPs in upstream. REFUEL features joint modeling learning, pseudo cumulative value
    function construction for exploration, joint exploration policy learning and a unique
    termination criterion. These novel designs enable REFUEL to leverage the shared representation
    across source tasks to improve the learning efficiency.

  • •
   
    We show that REFUEL can 1) find a near-optimal policy and 2) learn a near-accurate model for
    each of TT source tasks to certain average accuracy ϵu\epsilon_{u}, with a sample complexity of
    O~​(H3​d2​K2T​ϵu2+(H3​d4​K+H5​d​K2+H5​d3​K)ϵu2CLOSE\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{H^{3}d^{2}K^{2}}{T\
    epsilon_{u}^{2}}+\frac{\left(H^{3}d^{4}K+H^{5}dK^{2}+H^{5}d^{3}K\right)}{\epsilon_{u}^{2}}\
    right. OPEN+H5​K3d​T​ϵu2)\left.+\frac{H^{5}K^{3}}{dT\epsilon_{u}^{2}}\right), where H,K,dH,K,d
    denote the horizon steps, the cardinality of action space and the low-rank dimension,
    respectively. Compared with state-of-the-art single-task representation learning in low-rank
    MDPs (Uehara et al.,, 2021), REFUEL achieves reduced sample complexity as long as the number TT
    of tasks satisfies T=Ω⁡(Kd5)T=\Omega\left(\frac{K}{d^{5}}\right), which shows the benefit of
    multitask learning provably. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first theoretical result
    demonstrating the benefit of representation learning in exploration-based reward-free multitask
    RL.

  • •
   
    Finally, we apply the learned representation from REFUEL to downstream online and offline
    learning problems. In offline RL, we propose a new sample efficient algorithm with the
    suboptimality gap bounded by O~​(H​d​ξdown+H​max⁡{d,p}/Noff)\widetilde{O}(H\sqrt{d}\xi_{\mathrm
    {down}}+H\max\{d,\sqrt{p}\}/\sqrt{N_{\mathrm{off}}}), where NoffN_{\mathrm{off}} is the number
    of samples of offline dataset and pp measures the model complexity of reward. In online RL, we
    propose an online algorithm with the suboptimality gap bounded by O~(Hdξdown+Hd1/2No​n−1/2max
    {d,p})\widetilde{O}\left({Hd\xi_{\mathrm{down}}}+Hd^{1/2}N_{on}^{-1/2}\max\{d,\sqrt{p}\}\
    right), where NonN_{\mathrm{on}} is the number of iterations in the online algorithm. In both
    the offline and online settings, the first term captures the approximation error where ξdown\
    xi_{\mathrm{down}} measures the quality of the learned representation from upstream, and is
    negligible when the upstream learning is sufficiently accurate, and the second term improves
    the dependency on HH and dd compared with the state of the art (Uehara et al.,, 2021) for
    low-rank MDPs without the knowledge of the representation, showing the benefit of employing the
    learned representation from upstream.

Notations: Throughout the paper, we use [N][N] to denote set {1,…,N}\{1,\dots,N\} for N∈ℕN\in\
mathbb{N}, use ‖x‖2\left\lVert x\right\rVert_{2} to denote the ℓ2\ell_{2} norm of vector xx, use
‖X‖F\left\lVert X\right\rVert_{\mathrm{F}} to denote the Frobenius norm of matrix XX, use △⁡(𝒜)\
triangle(\mathcal{A}) to denote the probability simplex over set 𝒜\mathcal{A}, and use 𝒰⁡(𝒜)\
mathcal{U}(\mathcal{A}) to denote uniform sampling over 𝒜\mathcal{A}, given |𝒜|<∞|\mathcal{A}|<\
infty. Furthermore, for any symmetric positive definite matrix Σ\Sigma, we let ‖x‖Σ:=x⊤​Σ​x\left\
lVert x\right\rVert_{\Sigma}:=\sqrt{x^{\top}\Sigma x}.

2 Related work

Due to the rapid growth of theoretical studies in RL, we discuss only highly relevant work below.
Multitask bandits, multitask RL and meta-RL: The benefit of multitask learning in linear bandits
has been investigated in Yang et al., (2021); Qin et al., (2022); Cella et al., (2022); Azizi
et al., (2022); Deshmukh et al., (2017); Cella and Pontil, (2021); Hu et al., (2021). For multitask
RL, Arora et al., (2020) showed that representation learning can reduce sample complexity for
imitation learning. Calandriello et al., (2015) studied multitask learning under linear MDPs with
known representation. Hu et al., (2021) studied multitask RL with low inherent Bellman error (
Zanette et al., 2020a, ) with known representation. A concurrent work Lu et al., (2022) studied
multitask RL with low inherent Bellman error, bounded Eluder dimension and unknown representation.
Assuming that all tasks are sampled from an unknown distribution, Brunskill and Li, (2013) studied
the benefit of multitask RL when each task is sampled independently from a distribution over a
finite set of MDPs while Müller and Pacchiano, (2022) focused on meta-RL for linear mixture model (
Yang and Wang,, 2020). Considering all tasks share a common representation, D’Eramo et al., (2020)
showed the benefit in the convergence rate on value iteration and Lu et al., (2021) proved the
sample efficiency gain of multitask RL under low-rank MDPs. None of the above works took the
influence of sequential exploration and temporal dependence in data into consideration, where Lu
et al., (2021) assumed a generative model is accessible and D’Eramo et al., (2020) assumed that the
dataset is given in prior. In our study, we design reward-free exploration for multitask RL.
Single-task RL under tabular and linear MDPs: Offline RL under tabular MDPs was studied in Yang and
Wang, (2020); Yin and Wang, 2021b (); Xie et al., 2021b (); Ren et al., (2021); Yin and Wang, 2021a
(), where provably efficient algorithms were proposed. Further, offline RL under linear MDPs (with
the the known representation) was studied in Jin et al., 2021b (), where an algorithm called PEVI
was proposed and the suboptimality gap was characterized. Xie et al., 2021a () considered function
approximation under the Bellman-consistent assumptions and Zanette et al., (2021) considered the
linear Bellman complete model which can be specialized to the linear MDP. Yin et al., (2022)
studied offline RL under linear MDPs and leverages the variance information to improve the sample
complexity. Wang et al., 2021a (); Zanette, (2021) studied the statistical hardness of offline RL
with linear representations and provided the exponential lower bounds.

Online RL under tabular MDPs has been extensively studied for both model-based and model-free
settings (Dann et al.,, 2017; Strehl et al.,, 2006; Jin et al.,, 2018; Azar et al.,, 2017; Osband
et al.,, 2016; Jaksch et al.,, 2010), and with the access to a generative model (Koenig and
Simmons,, 1993; Azar et al.,, 2011; Lattimore and Hutter,, 2012; Sidford et al.,, 2018). A line of
recent studies focused on function approximation under linear MDPs with known representation and
proposed provably efficient algorithms (Jin et al.,, 2020; Zanette et al., 2020a, ; Zanette et al.,
2020b, ; Wang et al., 2021b, ; Neu and Pike-Burke,, 2020; Sun et al., 2019a, ; Cai et al.,, 2020).
Specifically, Jin et al., (2020) also studied the setting where the representation of the
transition probability has a small model misspecification error. We remark that although our
downstream online/offline RL also has a misspecification error, it is due to the estimation error
of the representation from upstream. Thus, our analysis of the suboptimality gap naturally relies
on bridging the connection between upstream and downstream performances, which is not the case in
Jin et al., (2020).

Single-task RL under low-rank MDPs: RL with Low-rank MDPs has been studied recently in Agarwal
et al., (2020) for the reward-free setting with the model-identification guarantee. Modi et al.,
(2021) proposed a model-free algorithm for reward-free RL under low-nonnegative-rank MDPs. Uehara
et al., (2021) proposed provably efficient model-based algorithms for both online and offline RL
with known reward. Du et al., (2019); Misra et al., (2020); Zhang et al., (2022) studied the block
MDP which is a special case of low-rank MDPs. Further, algorithms have been proposed for MDP models
with low Bellman rank (Jiang et al.,, 2017), low witness rank (Sun et al., 2019b, ), bilinear
classes (Du et al.,, 2021) and low Bellman eluder dimension (Jin et al., 2021a, ), which can be
specialized to low-rank MDPs. Those algorithms can achieve better sample complexity bound, but
contain computationally costly steps as remarked in Uehara et al., (2021). Moreover, Zhang et al.,
(2021) studied a slightly different low-rank model, and showed a problem dependent regret upper
bound for their proposed algorithm.

Comparison to concurrent work: After we submitted this work for potential publication in May 2022,
a concurrent work (Agarwal et al.,, 2022) was posted on arXiv. Both our work and Agarwal et al.,
(2022) study a common setting where the upstream learning is reward-free and has no access to a
generative model, and downstream online RL applies the learned representation from upstream.
However, the two studies differ substantially in upstream algorithm design and sample complexity
guarantee. First, one prominent difference in the upstream algorithm design is that our algorithm
REFUEL jointly learns policies among the source tasks for exploration, whereas their algorithm
learns the exploration policies for the source tasks individually. Second, for downstream online
RL, although both results achieve the same suboptimality gap Hd3/2Non−1/2Hd^{3/2}N_{\mathrm{on}}^
{-1/2}, our guarantee requires much fewer samples from upstream than theirs due to the joint policy
learning among source tasks and a different reward-free exploration scheme in our design. Further,
Agarwal et al., (2022) directly assumes policy-level model misspecification from upstream to
guarantee the downstream performance, whereas we make assumptions on the model classes Φ,Ψ\Phi,\
Psi, based on which we theoretically characterize the misspecification error of the learned
representation from upstream (Lemma 5.6).

We further note that our work and Agarwal et al., (2022) also study some non-overlapping topics,
such as downstream offline RL in our study, whereas upstream learning with generative models in
their study.

3 Problem formulation

3.1 Episodic MDP

Consider an episodic non-stationary Markov Decision Process (MDP) with finite horizon HH, denoted
by ℳ=(𝒮,𝒜,H,P,r)\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},H,P,r), where 𝒮\mathcal{S} is the state space
(possibly infinite), 𝒜\mathcal{A} is the finite action space with cardinality KK, HH is the number
of steps in each episode, P={Ph}h∈[H]P=\{P_{h}\}_{h\in[H]} is the collection of transition measures
with Ph:𝒮×𝒜↦△⁡(𝒮)P_{h}:\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\mapsto\triangle(\mathcal{S}), r={rh}h∈[H]r=\{r_
{h}\}_{h\in[H]} is the collection of deterministic reward functions with rh:𝒮×𝒜↦[0,1]r_{h}:\mathcal
{S}\times\mathcal{A}\mapsto[0,1]. The initial state s1{s}_{1} is assumed to be fixed for each
episode and the sum of the reward is normalized with ∑h=1Hrh∈[0,1]\sum_{h=1}^{H}r_{h}\in[0,1].

Denote policy π={πh}h∈[H]\pi=\{\pi_{h}\}_{h\in[H]} as a sequence of mappings where πh:𝒮↦△⁡(𝒜)\pi_
{h}:\mathcal{S}\mapsto\triangle(\mathcal{A}). We use πh​(a|s)\pi_{h}(a|s) to denote the probability
of selecting action aa in state ss at step hh. Given a starting state shs_{h}, we use sh′∼(P,π)s_{h
^{\prime}}\sim(P,\pi) to denote a state sampled by executing policy π\pi under the transition model
PP for h′−hh^{\prime}-h steps and 𝔼(sh,ah)∼(P,π)​[⋅]\mathbb{E}_{(s_{h},a_{h})\sim(P,\pi)}\left[\
cdot\right] to denote the expectation over states sh∼(P,π)s_{h}\sim(P,\pi) and actions ah∼πa_{h}\
sim\pi. Given policy π\pi and a state ss at step hh, the (state) value function is defined as
Vh,P,rπ​(s):=𝔼(sh′,ah′)∼(P,π)​[∑h′=hHrh′​(sh′,ah′)|sh=s]V_{h,P,r}^{\pi}(s):={\mathbb{E}}_{(s_{h^{\
prime}},a_{h^{\prime}})\sim(P,\pi)}\left[\sum_{h^{\prime}=h}^{H}r_{h^{\prime}}(s_{h^{\prime}},a_{h^
{\prime}})\middle|s_{h}=s\right]. Similarly, the (state-)action value function for a given
state-action pair (s,a)(s,a) under policy π\pi at step hh is defined as Qh,P,rπ​(s,a)=rh​(s,a)+
(Ph​Vh+1,P,rπ)​(s,a)Q_{h,P,r}^{\pi}(s,a)=r_{h}(s,a)+(P_{h}V_{h+1,P,r}^{\pi})(s,a), where (Phf)(s,a)
=𝔼s′∼Ph(⋅|s,a)[f(s′)](P_{h}f)(s,a)=\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim P_{h}(\cdot|s,a)}\left[f(s^{\prime})\
right] for any function f:𝒮↦ℝf:\mathcal{S}\mapsto\mathbb{R}. For simplicity, we omit subscript hh
for h=1h=1. Since the MDP begins with the same initial state s1s_{1}, to simplify the notation, we
use VP,rπV_{P,r}^{\pi} to denote V1,P,rπ​(s1)V_{1,P,r}^{\pi}(s_{1}) without causing ambiguity in
the following. Let P∗P^{*} denote the transition kernel of the underlying environment. Given a
reward function rr, there always exists an optimal policy π∗\pi^{*} that yields the optimal value
function VP∗,rπ∗=supπVP∗,rπV_{P^{*},r}^{\pi^{*}}=\sup_{\pi}V_{P^{*},r}^{\pi}, abbreviated as
VP∗,r∗V_{P^{*},r}^{*}.

This paper focuses on low-rank MDPs (Jiang et al.,, 2017; Agarwal et al.,, 2020) defined as
follows.

Definition 3.1 (Low-rank MDPs).

A transition kernel Ph∗:𝒮×𝒜→△⁡(𝒜)P_{h}^{*}:\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\triangle(\
mathcal{A}) admits a low-rank decomposition with dimension d∈ℕd\in\mathbb{N} if there exists two
embedding functions ϕh∗:𝒮×𝒜→ℝd\phi_{h}^{*}:\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
and μh∗:𝒮→ℝd\mu_{h}^{*}:\mathcal{S}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d} such that

 Ph∗​(s′|s,a)=⟨ϕh∗​(s,a),μh∗​(s′)⟩,∀s,s′∈𝒮,a∈𝒜.\displaystyle P_{h}^{*}(s^{\prime}|s,a)=\left\
 langle\phi_{h}^{*}(s,a),\mu_{h}^{*}(s^{\prime})\right\rangle,\quad\forall s,s^{\prime}\in\mathcal
 {S},a\in\mathcal{A}.

Without loss of generality, we assume ‖ϕh∗​(s,a)‖2≤1\left\lVert\phi_{h}^{*}(s,a)\right\rVert_{2}\
leq 1 for all (s,a)∈𝒮×𝒜(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A} and for any function g:𝒮↦[0,1]g:\
mathcal{S}\mapsto[0,1], ‖∫μh∗​(s)​g​(s)​𝑑s‖2≤d\left\lVert\int\mu^{*}_{h}(s)g(s)ds\right\rVert_{2}\
leq\sqrt{d}. An MDP is a low-rank MDP with dimension dd if for h∈[H]h\in[H], its transition kernel
Ph∗P_{h}^{*} admits a low-rank decomposition with dimension dd. Let ϕ∗={ϕh∗}h∈[H]\phi^{*}=\{\phi^
{*}_{h}\}_{h\in[H]} and μ∗={μh∗}h∈[H]\mu^{*}=\{\mu^{*}_{h}\}_{h\in[H]} be the embeddings for P∗P^
{*}, where ϕ∗\phi^{*} is also called representation in RL literature.

3.2 Reward-free multitask RL and its downstream learning

We consider a multitask RL consisting of reward-free upstream and reward-known downstream.

In reward-free multitask upstream learning, the agent is expected to conduct reward-free
exploration over TT source tasks, where all reward functions {rt}t∈[T]\{r^{t}\}_{t\in[T]} are
unknown. Each task t∈[T]t\in[T] is associated with a low-rank MDP ℳt=(𝒮,𝒜,H,Pt,rt)\mathcal{M}^{t}=
(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},H,P^{t},r^{t}). All TT tasks are identical except for (a) their transition
model PtP^{t}, which admits a low-rank decomposition with dimension dd: Pt​(s′|s,a)=⟨ϕ∗​(s′),μ(∗,t)
​(s,a)⟩P^{t}(s^{\prime}|s,a)=\langle\phi^{*}(s^{\prime}),\mu^{(*,t)}(s,a)\rangle for all t∈[T]t\in
[T] and (b) their reward rtr^{t}. Namely, all tasks share the same feature function ϕ∗\phi^{*}, but
may differ in μ(∗,t)\mu^{(*,t)} as well as reward rtr^{t}. The goal of upstream learning is to find
a near-optimal policy and a near-accurate model for any task t∈[T]t\in[T] and any reward function
rt,t∈[T]r^{t},t\in[T] via sufficient reward-free exploration, and output a well-learned
representation for downstream task. For each t∈[T]t\in[T], we use P(∗,t)P^{(*,t)} to denote the
true transition kernel of task tt.

In downstream learning, the agent is assigned with a new target task T+1T+1 associated with a
low-rank MDP ℳT+1=(𝒮,𝒜,H,PT+1,rT+1)\mathcal{M}^{T+1}=(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},H,P^{T+1},r^{T+1}).
The task shares the same 𝒮,𝒜,H\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},H and ϕ∗\phi^{*} with the TT upstream tasks,
but has a task-specific μ(∗,T+1)\mu^{(*,T+1)}. We also assume that the reward for the new task
rT+1r^{T+1} is given to the agent. Here, the agent is assumed to take the learned representation
function ϕ^\widehat{\phi} during the upstream training, and then interacts with the new task
environment ℳT+1\mathcal{M}^{T+1} in an online or offline manner to find a near-optimal policy for
the new task. By utilizing the representation learned in the upstream, the agent is expected to
achieve better sample efficiency in the downstream. Finally, we use P(∗,T+1)P^{(*,T+1)} to denote
the true transition kernel of task T+1T+1.

4 Upstream reward-free multitask RL

In this section, we present our proposed algorithm for upstream reward-free multitask RL in
low-rank MDPs and characterize its theoretical performance.

Since it is impossible to learn a model in polynomial time if there is no assumption on features ϕh
\phi_{h} and μh\mu_{h}, we first adopt the following conventional assumption (Agarwal et al.,, 2020
).

Assumption 4.1.

(Realizability). A learning agent can access to a model class {(Φ,Ψ)}\{(\Phi,\Psi)\} that contains
the true model, namely, for any h∈[H],t∈[T]h\in[H],t\in[T], the embeddings ϕh∗∈Φ,μh(∗,t)∈Ψ\phi_{h}^
{*}\in\Phi,\mu_{h}^{(*,t)}\in\Psi.

While we assume the cardinality of function classes to be finite for simplicity, extensions to
infinite classes with bounded statistical complexity are not difficult (Sun et al., 2019b, ;
Agarwal et al.,, 2020).


Algorithm 1 REFUEL (REward Free mUltitask rEpresentation Learning)
1:  Input: Regularizer λn\lambda_{n}, parameter α~n{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{n}, ζn\zeta_{n},B , δ\
delta, ϵu\epsilon_{u}, Models {(μ,ϕ):μ∈Ψ,ϕ∈Φ}\{(\mu,\phi):\mu\in\Psi,\phi\in\Phi\}.
2:  Initialize π0(⋅|s)\pi_{0}(\cdot|s) to be uniform, set 𝒟h(0,t)=∅\mathcal{D}_{h}^{(0,t)}=\
emptyset for h∈[H],t∈[T]h\in[H],t\in[T].
3:  for n=1,…,Nun=1,\ldots,N_{u} do
4:   for h=1,…,Hh=1,\ldots,H do
5:    for t=1,…,Tt=1,\ldots,T do
6:     Under MDP ℳt\mathcal{M}^{t}, use πtn−1\pi_{t}^{n-1} to roll into sh−1(n,t,h)s_{h-1}^

{(n,t,h)}) to the dataset 𝒟h(n,t)=𝒟h(n−1,t)∪{(sh(n,t,h),ah(n,t,h),sh+1(n,t,h))}\mathcal{D}_{h}^

4.1 Algorithm design

We first describe our proposed algorithm REFUEL (REward Free mUltitask rEpresentation Learning)
depicted in Algorithm 1.

In each iteration nn, for each task tt and time step hh, the agent executes the exploration policy
πtn−1\pi_{t}^{n-1} followed by two uniformly chosen actions to collect trajectories for HH
episodes. Note that πtn−1\pi_{t}^{n-1} is designed at the previous iteration. Then, to utilize the
common feature of data collected from different source tasks, the agent processes the data jointly
as following steps.

Joint MLE-based Modeling Learning: The agent passes all previously collected data to estimate the
low-rank components ϕ^h(n),μ^h(n,1),…,μ^h(n,t)\widehat{\phi}_{h}^{(n)},\widehat{\mu}_{h}^{(n,1)},\
ldots,\widehat{\mu}_{h}^{(n,t)} simultaneously via the MLE oracle M​L​E​(⋃t∈[T]𝒟h(n,t))MLE\left(\
bigcup_{t\in[T]}\mathcal{D}_{h}^{(n,t)}\right) (line 9) on the joint distribution defined as
follows:

 (ϕ^h(n),μ^h(n,1),…,μ^h(n,T))=arg⁡maxϕh∈Φ,μh1,…,μhT∈Ψ⁡∑n=1Nlog⁡(∏t=1T⟨ϕh​(sh(n,t,h),ah
 (n,t,h)),μht​(sh+1(n,t,h))⟩).\displaystyle\left(\hskip-0.72229pt\widehat{\phi}_{h}^{(n)}\
 hskip-2.168pt,\widehat{\mu}_{h}^{(n,1)}\hskip-2.168pt,\ldots,\widehat{\mu}_{h}^{(n,T)}\
 hskip-0.72229pt\right)=\hskip-7.22743pt\mathop{\arg\max}_{\phi_{h}\in\Phi,\mu_{h}^{1},\ldots,  (1)
 \mu_{h}^{T}\in\Psi}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\hskip-0.72229pt\log\hskip-0.72229pt\left(\hskip-0.72229pt\
 prod_{t=1}^{T}\langle\phi_{h}(s_{h}^{(n,t,h)}\hskip-2.168pt,a_{h}^{(n,t,h)}),\mu_{h}^{t}(s_
 {h+1}^{(n,t,h)})\rangle\hskip-0.72229pt\right).

The MLE oracle above generalizes the MLE oracle commonly adopted for single-task RL in the
literature (Agarwal et al.,, 2020; Uehara et al.,, 2021). In practice, MLE oracle can be
efficiently implemented if the model classes Φ,Ψ\Phi,\Psi are properly parameterized such as by
neural networks.

Next, for each task tt, the agent uses the learned embeddings ϕ^h(n),μ^h(n,t)\widehat{\phi}_{h}^
{(n)},\widehat{\mu}_{h}^{(n,t)} to update the estimated transition kernel P^(n,t)\widehat{P}^
{(n,t)} at each step hh as

 P^h(n,t)​(s′|s,a)=⟨ϕ^h(n)​(s,a),μ^h(n,t)​(s′)⟩.\displaystyle\textstyle\widehat{P}_{h}^{(n,t)}
 (s^{\prime}|s,a)=\langle\widehat{\phi}_{h}^{(n)}(s,a),\widehat{\mu}_{h}^{(n,t)}(s^{\prime})\   (2)
 rangle.

Pseudo Cumulative Value Function Construction for Exploration: The agent first uses the
representation estimator ϕ^h(n)\widehat{\phi}_{h}^{(n)} to update the empirical covariance matrix U
^h(n,t)\widehat{U}_{h}^{(n,t)} as

 U^h(n,t)=∑τ=1nϕ^h(n)​(sh(τ,t,h+1),ah(τ,t,h+1))​(ϕ^h(n)​(sh(τ,t,h+1),ah(τ,t,h+1)))⊤+λn​I,\
 displaystyle\textstyle\widehat{U}_{h}^{(n,t)}=\sum_{\tau=1}^{n}{\widehat{\phi}}_{h}^{(n)}(s_   (3)
 {h}^{(\tau,t,h+1)},a_{h}^{(\tau,t,h+1)})(\widehat{\phi}_{h}^{(n)}(s_{h}^{(\tau,t,h+1)},a_{h}^
 {(\tau,t,h+1)}))^{\top}+\lambda_{n}I,

where {sh(τ,t,h+1),ah(τ,t,h+1),sh+1(τ,t,h+1)}\{s_{h}^{(\tau,t,h+1)},a_{h}^{(\tau,t,h+1)},s_{h+1}^
{(\tau,t,h+1)}\} is the tuple collected at the τ\tau-th iteration, (h+1)(h+1)-th episode, and step
hh. Then, the agent uses both ϕ^h(n)\widehat{\phi}_{h}^{(n)} and U^h(n,t)\widehat{U}_{h}^{(n,t)} to
provide an exploration-driven reward as

 b^h(n,t)​(sh,ah)=min⁡{α~n​‖ϕ^h(n)​(sh,ah)‖(U^h(n,t))−1,B},\displaystyle\textstyle\widehat{b}_
 {h}^{(n,t)}(s_{h},a_{h})=\min\left\{{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{n}\left\|\widehat{\phi}_{h}^{(n)}    (4)
 (s_{h},a_{h})\right\|_{(\widehat{U}^{(n,t)}_{h})^{-1}},B\right\},

where BB and α~n{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{n} are pre-determined parameters. To integrate information of
learned models and exploration-driven rewards for TT tasks, we define a Pseudo Cumulative Value
Function (PCV) as

 P​C​V​(P^(n,t),b^h(n,t),πt,T)=∑h=1H−1∑t=1T{𝔼sh∼(P^(n,t),πt)ah∼πt[b^h(n,t)​(sh,ah)]}2.\
 displaystyle\textstyle PCV\left(\widehat{P}^{(n,t)},\widehat{b}_{h}^{(n,t)},\pi_{t};T\right)=
 \sum_{h=1}^{H-1}\sqrt{\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left\{\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{s_{h}\sim(\widehat{P}^         (5)
 {(n,t)},\pi_{t})\atop a_{h}\sim\pi_{t}}\left[\widehat{b}_{h}^{(n,t)}(s_{h},a_{h})\right]\
 right\}^{2}}.

Joint Policy Learning for Exploration: The agent then learns TT policies via optimization over the
PCV, and uses these policies as the exploration policy for each task in the next iteration. Since
PCV measures the cumulative model estimation error over all tasks, these policies will explore the
state-action space where the model estimation has large uncertainty on average of TT tasks. Here we
adopt an optimization oracle for line 15 in Algorithm 1.

Termination: REFUEL is equipped with a termination condition and outputs the current estimated
model and representation during iterations if the PCV plus certain minor term is below a threshold,
which suffices to guarantee that for each task tt, the returned model P^(t)\widehat{P}^{(t)} from
REFUEL reaches an average proximity to the true model P(∗,t)P^{(*,t)}.

The joint learning and decision making among all tasks involved in the four main components
differentiate REFUEL from single-task RL algorithms, and enable it to exploit the shared
representation across the source tasks to improve the sample efficiency.

4.2 Theoretical results on sample complexity

In this section, we first establish the sample complexity upper bound for REFUEL and then compare
our multitask result with that of single-task RL.

Theorem 4.2.

For any fixed δ∈(0,|Ψ|−min⁡{T,Kd2})\delta\in(0,{|\Psi|^{-\min\{T,\frac{K}{d^{2}}\}}}), set λn=O⁡
(d​log⁡(|Φ|​n​T​H/δ))\lambda_{n}=O(d\log(|\Phi|nTH/\delta)), ζn=O⁡(log⁡(|Φ|​|Ψ|T​n​H/δ)/n)\zeta_{n}
=O({\log\left(|\Phi||\Psi|^{T}nH/\delta\right)}/{n}), α~n=O⁡(K​log⁡(|Φ|​|Ψ|T​n​H/δ)+λn​d​T){\
widetilde{\alpha}}_{n}=O(\sqrt{K\log(|\Phi||\Psi|^{T}nH/\delta)+\lambda_{n}dT}) and B=O⁡(T+K/d2)B=O
(\sqrt{T+K/d^{2}}). Let ϕ^,P^(1),…,P^(T)\widehat{\phi},\widehat{P}^{(1)},\ldots,\widehat{P}^{(T)}
be the output of Algorithm 1. Then, under Assumption 4.1, with probability at least 1−δ1-\delta,
for any policy πt\pi_{t} of task t∈[T]t\in[T] and any step h∈[H]h\in[H], we have

 1T∑t=1T\displaystyle 𝔼sh∼(P(∗,t),πt),ah∼πt[‖P^h(t)(⋅|sh,ah)−Ph(∗,t)(⋅|sh,ah)‖T​V]≤ϵu.\
 \textstyle\frac{1}   displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{s_{h}\sim(P^{(*,t)},\pi_{t}),a_{h}\sim\pi_{t}}\   (6)
 {T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}    left[\left\|\widehat{P}_{h}^{(t)}(\cdot|s_{h},a_{h})-P_{h}^{(*,t)}(\cdot
                      |s_{h},a_{h})\right\|_{TV}\right]\leq\epsilon_{u}.

Further, for any task tt, given any reward rtr^{t}, let π^t=arg⁡maxπ⁡VP^(t),rtπ\widehat{\pi}_{t}={\
arg\max}_{\pi}V_{\widehat{P}^{(t)},r^{t}}^{\pi}. Then with probability at least 1−δ1-\delta, 1T​∑t=
1T[VP(∗,t),rt∗−VP(∗,t),rtπ^t]≤ϵu\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}[V_{P^{(*,t)},r^{t}}^{*}-V_{P^{(*,t)},r^
{t}}^{\widehat{\pi}_{t}}]\leq\epsilon_{u}.

Meanwhile, the number of trajectories collected by each task is at most

 O~​(H3​d2​K2T​ϵu2+(H3​d4​K+H5​d​K2+H5​d3​K)ϵu2+H5​K3d​T​ϵu2).\textstyle\widetilde{O}\left(\
 frac{H^{3}d^{2}K^{2}}{T\epsilon_{u}^{2}}+\frac{\left(H^{3}d^{4}K+H^{5}dK^{2}+H^{5}d^{3}K\      (7)
 right)}{\epsilon_{u}^{2}}+\frac{H^{5}K^{3}}{dT\epsilon_{u}^{2}}\right).

Equation 6 in Theorem 4.2 indicates that the estimated transition kernels of the TT MDPs meet the
required accuracy on average. Theorem 4.2 further indicates that with the data collected during
such reward-free exploration, if every task is further given any reward function, near-optimal
policies can be found for all tasks on average as well.

For the sample complexity bound in Equation 7, the first term is inversely proportional to TT,
which captures sample-benefit for each task due to learning the TT models jointly. The second term
is related to the number of samples to guarantee the concentration of empirical covariance matrix U
^h(n,t)\widehat{U}_{h}^{(n,t)} in order to identify desirable exploration policies. Such
concentration needs to be satisfied for each task tt independently and can not be implemented
jointly, which makes the second term independent with TT. The third term originally arises from the
model estimation error shift among TT tasks when implementing joint MLE-based model estimation. In
the algorithm, this error shift is then transferred to BB defined in Equation 4, which provides an
explicit upper bound for the exploration-driven reward b^h(n,t)\widehat{b}_{h}^{(n,t)}. Note that b
^h(n,t)\widehat{b}_{h}^{(n,t)} is involved in the PCV to guide exploration, which finally
contributes to convergence of REFUEL and affects its sample complexity.

Compared with the state-of-the-art sample complexity that scales in O~​(H5​d4​K2ϵu2)\widetilde{O}\
left(\frac{H^{5}d^{4}K^{2}}{\epsilon_{u}^{2}}\right)^1^1 1 We convert their results in discounted
setting to our episodic MDP setting by replacing 1/(1−γ)=Θ⁡(H)1/(1-\gamma)=\Theta(H). when
performing TT single-task RL independently (Uehara et al.,, 2021), REFUEL achieves lower sample
complexity when T=Ω⁡(Kd5)T=\Omega\left(\frac{K}{d^{5}}\right), and it reduces the single-task
sample complexity in Uehara et al., (2021) by an order of O⁡(min⁡{H2​d2​T,H2​K,d3,d​K,d5​TK})O\left
(\min\{{H^{2}d^{2}T},{H^{2}K},{d^{3}},{dK},\frac{d^{5}T}{K}\}\right).

Technically, the joint learning feature of REFUEL requires new analytical techniques. We outline
the main steps of the proof of Theorem 4.2 here and defer the detailed proof to Appendix A. Step 1:
We develop a new upper bound on model estimation error for each task, which captures the advantage
of joint MLE model estimation over single-task learning, as shown in Proposition A.1. Step 2: We
establish the PCV as an uncertainty measure that captures the difference between the estimated and
ground truth models. This justifies using such a PCV as a guidance for further exploration, as
shown in Proposition A.3. Step 3: We show that the summation of the PCVs over all iterations is
sublinear with respect to the total number NuN_{u} of iterations, as shown in Proposition A.4,
which further implies polynomial efficiency of REFUEL in learning the models.

5 Downstream RL

In downstream RL, the agent is given a new target task T+1T+1 under MDP ℳT+1\mathcal{M}^{T+1} and
aims to find a near-optimal policy for it. The downstream task is assumed to share the same
representation as the upstream tasks, and hence the agent can adopt the representation learned from
the upstream to expedite its learning. Since the agent does not know the task-specific embedding
function μT+1\mu^{T+1}, the agent is allowed to interact with MDP ℳT+1\mathcal{M}^{T+1} in an
online RL setting or have access to an offline dataset 𝒟down={(shτ,ahτ,rhτ,sh+1τ)}τ,h=1Noff,H\
mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{down}}=\{(s_{h}^{\tau},a_{h}^{\tau},r_{h}^{\tau},s_{h+1}^{\tau})\}_{\tau,h=1}^
{N_{\mathrm{off}},H} in an offline RL setting, which is rolled out from some behavior policy ρ\rho.

In this section, we first introduce the connections between upstream and downstream MDPs in Section
 5.1. We then provide algorithms for the downstream task and characterize their suboptimality gap
for offline RL in Sections 5.2 and 5.3, respectively, and for online RL in Sections 5.4 and 5.5,
respectively.

5.1 Connections between upstream and downstream MDPs

In downstream RL tasks, the agent will adopt the feature ϕ^\widehat{\phi} learned from upstream
Algorithm 1. To ensure that the feature learned in upstream can still work well in downstream
tasks, upstream and downstream MDPs should have certain connections. We next elaborate some
reasonable assumptions on transition kernels to build such connections.

The following reachability assumption is common in relevant literature (Modi et al.,, 2021).

Assumption 5.1 (Reachability).

For each source task tt in upstream, whose transition kernel is P(∗,t)P^{(*,t)}, there exists a
policy πt0\pi_{t}^{0} such that mins∈𝒮⁡ℙh(πt0,t)​(s)≥κu\min_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\mathbb{P}^{(\pi_{t}^
{0},t)}_{h}(s)\geq\kappa_{u}, where ℙh(πt0,t)​(⋅):𝒮→ℝ\mathbb{P}^{(\pi_{t}^{0},t)}_{h}(\cdot):\
mathcal{S}\rightarrow\mathbb{R} is the density function over 𝒮\mathcal{S} using policy πt0\pi_{t}^
{0} to roll into state ss at timestep hh.

Assumption 5.2.

Assume the state space 𝒮\mathcal{S} is compact and has finite measure 1/υ1/\upsilon. Hence, the
uniform distribution on 𝒮\mathcal{S} has the density function f⁡(s)=υf(s)=\upsilon.

Under Assumption 5.2, we denote 𝒰⁡(𝒮)\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{S}) as the uniform distribution over 𝒮\
mathcal{S} and 𝒰⁡(𝒮,𝒜)\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A}) as the uniform distribution over 𝒮×𝒜\
mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}.

Assumption 5.3.

For any two different models in the model class {(Φ,Ψ)}\{(\Phi,\Psi)\}, say P1​(s′|s,a)=⟨ϕ1​
(s,a),μ1​(s′)⟩P^{1}(s^{\prime}|s,a)=\left\langle\phi^{1}(s,a),\mu^{1}(s^{\prime})\right\rangle and
P2​(s′|s,a)=⟨ϕ2​(s,a),μ2​(s′)⟩P^{2}(s^{\prime}|s,a)=\left\langle\phi^{2}(s,a),\mu^{2}(s^{\prime})\
right\rangle, there exists a constant CRC_{R} such that for all (s,a)∈𝒮×𝒜(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\
mathcal{A} and h∈[H]h\in[H],

  ∥Ph1(⋅|s,a)−Ph2(⋅|s,a)∥T​V≤CR𝔼(sh,ah)∼𝒰⁡(𝒮,𝒜)∥Ph1(⋅|sh,ah)−Ph2(⋅|s2,a2)∥T​V.\displaystyle\|P
  ^{1}_{h}(\cdot|s,a)-P^{2}_{h}(\cdot|s,a)\|_{TV}\leq C_{R}\mathbb{\mathbb{E}}_{(s_{h},a_{h})\  (8)
  sim\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A})}{\|P^{1}_{h}(\cdot|s_{h},a_{h})-P^{2}_{h}(\cdot|s_
  {2},a_{2})\|_{TV}}.

For normalization, we assume that for any ϕ∈Φ\phi\in\Phi, ‖ϕ⁡(s,a)‖2≤1\left\lVert\phi(s,a)\right\
rVert_{2}\leq 1 and for any μ∈Ψ\mu\in\Psi and any function g:𝒮→[0,1],‖∫μh​(s)​g​(s)​𝑑s‖2≤dg:\
mathcal{S}\rightarrow[0,1],\left\lVert\int\mu_{h}(s)g(s)ds\right\rVert_{2}\leq\sqrt{d}.

Assumption 5.3 ensures that for each source task tt and any (s,a)∈𝒮×𝒜(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\
mathcal{A}, the total variation distance between the learned P^(t)(⋅|s,a)\widehat{P}^{(t)}(\cdot|
s,a) and true transition kernels P^(∗,t)(⋅|s,a)\widehat{P}^{(*,t)}(\cdot|s,a) will not be too large
when the expectation of the total variation distance over the entire state action space is small.

Assumption 5.4.

For the underlying MDP of task T+1T+1, we assume that the reward function rT+1r^{T+1} is chosen
from a function class ℛ\mathcal{R} with ε\varepsilon-covering number 𝒩ℛ​(ε)\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal
{R}}(\varepsilon) for any given ε\varepsilon, and 𝒩ℛ​(ε)=O~​(1εp)\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}(\
varepsilon)=\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{p}}\right). ). The true transition kernel P
(∗,T+1)P^{(*,T+1)} can be ξ\xi-approximated by a linear combination of TT source tasks, i.e. there
exist TT (unknown) coefficients c1,…,cT∈[0,CL]c_{1},\ldots,c_{T}\in[0,C_{L}] and ξ≥0\xi\geq 0 such
that

 ∀(s,a)∈𝒮×𝒜,h∈[H],‖P(∗,T+1)(⋅|s,a)−∑t=1TctP(∗,t)(⋅|s,a)‖T​V≤ξ.\displaystyle\textstyle\forall
 (s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A},h\in[H],\quad\left\|P^{(*,T+1)}(\cdot|s,a)-\sum_{t=1}^    (9)
 {T}c_{t}P^{(*,t)}(\cdot|s,a)\right\|_{TV}\leq\xi.

Furthermore, ξ\xi is called the linear combination misspecification.

Assumption 5.4 establishes the underlying connection between upstream source tasks and the
downstream target task. The assumption on 𝒩ε​(ℛ)\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}(\mathcal{R}) is more
general than the common assumption on reward under linear MDP and can be easily achieved in
practice. For example, if the reward is linear with respect to an unknown feature ϕ~:𝒮×𝒜→ℝp\
widetilde{\phi}:\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{p}, i.e., there exists θ∈ℝp\
theta\in\mathbb{R}^{p} and ‖θ‖≤R\left\lVert\theta\right\rVert\leq R, r⁡(s,a)=⟨ϕ~​(s,a),θ⟩r(s,a)=\
langle\widetilde{\phi}(s,a),\theta\rangle, then 𝒩ε​(ℛ)=O~​((1+2​Rε)p)\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}(\
mathcal{R})=\widetilde{O}\left(\left(1+\frac{2R}{\varepsilon}\right)^{p}\right)(see Lemma D.5 in
Jin et al., (2020)), which satisfies the assumption. We remark here ϕ~\widetilde{\phi} is not
necessarily the same as ϕ∗\phi^{*}, and even its dimension pp can be different from dd.

We then introduce the definition of ϵ\epsilon-approximate linear MDP, and provide a guarantee for
the estimated feature.

Definition 5.5 (ϵ\epsilon-approximate linear MDP).

For any ϵ>0\epsilon>0, MDP ℳ=(𝒮,𝒜,H,P,r)\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},H,P,r) is an ϵ\
epsilon-approximate linear MDP with a time-dependent feature map ϕ:𝒮×𝒜→ℝd\phi:\mathcal{S}\times\
mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d} if, for any h∈[H]h\in[H], there exist a time-dependent unknown
(signed) measures μ\mu over 𝒮\mathcal{S} such that for any (s,a)∈𝒮×𝒜(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\
mathcal{A}, we have

 ‖Ph(⋅|s,a)−⟨ϕh(s,a),μh(⋅)⟩‖T​V≤ϵ.\displaystyle\left\|P_{h}(\cdot|s,a)-\left\langle\phi_{h}    (10)
 (s,a),\mu_{h}(\cdot)\right\rangle\right\|_{TV}\leq\epsilon.

Any ϕ\phi satisfying Equation 10 is called an ϵ\epsilon-approximate feature map for ℳ\mathcal{M}.

Let ξd​o​w​n=ξ+CL​CR​T​υ​ϵuκu\xi_{down}=\xi+\frac{C_{L}C_{R}T\upsilon\epsilon_{u}}{\kappa_{u}}. The
following lemma shows that the feature ϕ^\widehat{\phi} learned in upstream is a ξd​o​w​n\xi_{down}
-approximate feature map and can approximate the true feature in the new task.

Lemma 5.6.

Under 5.1, 5.2, 5.3 and 5.4, the output of Algorithm 1 ϕ^\widehat{\phi} is a ξdown\xi_{\mathrm
{down}}-approximate feature for MDP ℳT+1=(𝒮,𝒜,H,P(∗,T+1),r,s1)\mathcal{M}^{T+1}=(\mathcal{S},\
mathcal{A},H,P^{(*,T+1)},r,s_{1}), i.e. there exist a time-dependent unknown (signed) measure μ^∗\
widehat{\mu}^{*} over 𝒮\mathcal{S} such that for any (s,a)∈𝒮×𝒜(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A},
we have

 ‖Ph(∗,T+1)(⋅|s,a)−⟨ϕ^h(s,a),μ^h∗(⋅)⟩‖T​V≤ξd​o​w​n.\displaystyle\textstyle\left\|P_{h}^
 {(*,T+1)}(\cdot|s,a)-\left\langle\widehat{\phi}_{h}(s,a),\widehat{\mu}_{h}^{*}(\cdot)\right\  (11)
 rangle\right\|_{TV}\leq\xi_{down}.

Furthermore, for any g:𝒮→[0,1]g:\mathcal{S}\rightarrow[0,1], ‖∫μ^h∗​(s)​g​(s)​𝑑s‖2≤CL​d\left\lVert\
int\widehat{\mu}^{*}_{h}(s)g(s)ds\right\rVert_{2}\leq C_{L}\sqrt{d}.

5.2 Algorithm for downstream offline RL

We present our pessimistic value iteration algorithm for downstream offline RL in Algorithm 2.
Although our algorithm shares similar design principles as traditional algorithms for linear MDPs (
Jin et al., 2021b, ), it differs from them significantly as described in the following, due to the
misspecification of representation from upstream and the general rather than linear reward function
adopted. For ease of exposition, we define Bellman operator 𝔹h\mathbb{B}_{h} as (𝔹h​f)​(s,a)=rh​
(s,a)+(Ph(∗,T+1)​f)​(s,a)(\mathbb{B}_{h}f)(s,a)=r_{h}(s,a)+(P_{h}^{(*,T+1)}f)(s,a) for any
f:𝒮×𝒜↦ℝf:\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\mapsto\mathbb{R}. The main body of the algorithm consists of
a backward iteration over steps. In each iteration hh, the agent executes the following main steps.

Approximate Bellman update with general reward function. We construct an estimated Bellman update 𝔹
^h​V^h+1\widehat{\mathbb{B}}_{h}\widehat{V}_{h+1} based on the dataset 𝒟down\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm
{down}} to approximate 𝔹h​V^h+1\mathbb{B}_{h}\widehat{V}_{h+1}. Here V^h+1\widehat{V}_{h+1} is an
estimated value function constructed in the previous iteration based on 𝒟down\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm
{down}}. Since our reward function is more general and not necessarily linear in the feature, we
choose to parameterized Ph(∗,T+1)​Vh+1∗P_{h}^{(*,T+1)}V^{*}_{h+1} instead of Qh∗​(s,a)=rh​(s,a)+(Ph
(∗,T+1)​Vh+1∗)​(s,a)Q_{h}^{*}(s,a)=r_{h}(s,a)+(P_{h}^{(*,T+1)}V_{h+1}^{*})(s,a) due to the linear
structure of Ph(∗,T+1)P_{h}^{(*,T+1)}. Although the ground truth representation is unknown, the
agent is able to use the estimated representation learned from the upstream learning to
parameterize the linear term (Ph(∗,T+1)​Vh+1∗)​(s,a)(P_{h}^{(*,T+1)}V_{h+1}^{*})(s,a). Then, the
coefficient wh∗w_{h}^{*} can be estimated by solving the following regularized least squares
problem:

which has the closed-from solution w^h=Λh−1​∑τ=1Noffϕ^h​(shτ,ahτ)​V^h+1​(sh+1τ)\widehat{w}_{h}=\

^h​(⋅,⋅)(\widehat{\mathbb{B}}_{h}\widehat{V}_{h+1})(\cdot,\cdot)=r_{h}(\cdot,\cdot)+\widehat{w}_{h}
^{\top}\widehat{\phi}_{h}(\cdot,\cdot).

Design pessimism for incorporating upstream misspecification of representation. Pessimism is
realized by subtracting an uncertainty metric Γh\Gamma_{h} from Q^h\widehat{Q}_{h} (line 5). The
uncertainty metric Γh\Gamma_{h} quantifies the Bellman update error |(𝔹^h​V^h+1−𝔹h​V^h+1)​(s,a)||(\
widehat{\mathbb{B}}_{h}\widehat{V}_{h+1}-\mathbb{B}_{h}\widehat{V}_{h+1})(s,a)| at step hh. Our
selection of Γh\Gamma_{h} is in the form of ξdown+β​(ϕ^h​Λh−1​ϕ^h)1/2\xi_{\mathrm{down}}+\beta(\
widehat{\phi}_{h}\Lambda_{h}^{-1}\widehat{\phi}_{h})^{1/2}, which captures both the approximation
error (first term) and the estimation error in the Bellman update (second term). Both of the above
errors are affected by the misspecification of representation from upstream learning. Intuitively,
m:=(ϕ^h​Λh−1​ϕ^h)−1m:=(\widehat{\phi}_{h}\Lambda_{h}^{-1}\widehat{\phi}_{h})^{-1} represents the
effective number of samples the agent has explored along the ϕ^\widehat{\phi} direction, and the
penalty term β/m\beta/\sqrt{m} represents the uncertainty along the ϕ^\widehat{\phi} direction. By
choosing a proper value for β\beta, we can prove that with high probability, the uncertainty metric
Γh\Gamma_{h} always upper bounds the Bellman update error.

Select greedy policy. Line 6 executes a greedy policy π^h\widehat{\pi}_{h} to maximize Q^h\widehat
{Q}_{h}, which will be the output of the algorithm after the backward iteration over steps
completes.

5.3 Sample complexity for downstream offline RL

We first introduce the following coverage assumption for the estimated feature qualifying the
offline dataset, which can be easily checked in practice.

There exists absolute constant κρ\kappa_{\rho} such that

To the best of our knowledge, Theorem 5.8 provides the first suboptimality gap for offline RL where
the representation has an approximation error. The first result in Theorem 5.8 characterizes the
suboptimality gap if the offline data is rolled out from a behavior policy^2^2 2 The result remains
valid if offline data is compliant with MDP ℳT+1\mathcal{M}^{T+1} (Assumption 2.2 in Jin et al.,
2021b ()).. If the offline data further satisfies the feature coverage assumption, we are able to
upper bound the suboptimality gap by three terms in Equation 14. Such a result can be further
simplified as O~(Hd1/2ξdown+Noff−1/2Hmax{d,p})\widetilde{O}\left(Hd^{1/2}\xi_{\mathrm{down}}+N_{\
mathrm{off}}^{-1/2}H\max\{d,\sqrt{p}\}\right), where the first term is a constant error that arises
from the approximation error in Bellman update. The second term diminishes as NoffN_{\mathrm{off}}
grows to infinity, and it arises from the estimation error of w^h\widehat{w}_{h} in regularized
linear regression. The dependence on dd and p\sqrt{p} arises from the model complexity of the
linear function approximation class when we seek concentration of the estimated value function.

Compared to standard linear MDP with the knowledge of ground truth representation Jin et al., 2021b
(), our suboptimality gap contains an additional upstream misspecification error (i.e., ξdown\xi_{\
mathrm{down}}). The remaining term yields an order of O~(HdNoff−1/2)\widetilde{O}(HdN_{\mathrm
{off}}^{-1/2}) which matches that in Jin et al., 2021b () if we specialize our reward to be linear
with p=O⁡(d)p=O(d).

Theorem 5.8 also demonstrates that our downstream offline RL benefits from the learned
representation in upstream. As shown in Equation 15, if the linear combination misspecification
error ξ\xi is small enough, and the number of trajectories collected in upstream learning is
sufficient large, then the suboptimality gap is dominated by the term O~(Noff−1/2Hmax{d,p})\
widetilde{O}\left(N_{\mathrm{off}}^{-1/2}H\max\{d,\sqrt{p}\}\right), which improves O~(Noff−1/
2H2d2)\widetilde{O}(N_{\mathrm{off}}^{-1/2}H^{2}d^{2})^3^3 3 We convert their results in discounted
setting to our episodic MDP setting by replacing 1/(1−γ)=Θ⁡(H)1/(1-\gamma)=\Theta(H) and from PAC
bound to suboptimality gap. of REP-LCB (Uehara et al.,, 2021) under low-rank MDP with unknown
representation, with better dependence on HH and dd due to the benefit of upstream representation
learning.

5.4 Algorithm for downstream online RL

1:  Input: Feature ϕ^\widehat{\phi}, parameters λ\lambda, βn\beta_{n}.
2:  for n=1,…,Nn=1,\ldots,N do
3:   Receive the initial state s1n=s1s_{1}^{n}=s_{1}.
4:   for h=H,…,1h=H,\ldots,1 do
5:    Λhn=∑τ=1n−1ϕ^h​(shτ,ahτ)​ϕ^h​(shτ,ahτ)⊤+λ​Id\Lambda_{h}^{n}=\sum_{\tau=1}^{n-1}\widehat{\phi}

^{n-1}\widehat{\phi}_{h}(s_{h}^{\tau},a_{h}^{\tau})V_{h+1}^{n}(s_{h+1}^{\tau}).

8:   end for
9:   Let πn\pi^{n} be the greedy policy induced by {Qhn}h=1H\{Q_{h}^{n}\}_{h=1}^{H}, i.e., πhn​(⋅)=
arg⁡maxa∈𝒜​Qhn​(⋅,a)\pi_{h}^{n}(\cdot)={\arg\max}_{a\in\mathcal{A}}Q_{h}^{n}(\cdot,a)
10:   for h=1,…,Hh=1,\ldots,H do
11:    Take action ahn=πn​(shn)a_{h}^{n}=\pi^{n}(s_{h}^{n}), and observe sh+1ns_{h+1}^{n}.
12:   end for
13:  end for
14:  Output: π1,…,πn\pi^{1},\ldots,\pi^{n}.

Algorithm 3 Downstream Online RL

We present our downstream online RL algorithm in Algorithm 3, where the agent is allowed to
interact with the new task environment for policy optimization, and utilizes the learned feature
from upstream to implement linear approximation for state-action value function with traditional
UCB type of algorithms.

The algorithm consists of three iterations, one outer iteration and two inner backward iterations
over steps. Construct optimistic action value function: In each inner backward iteration, the agent
first constructs the empirical feature covariance matrices and estimates the weights w^\widehat{w}
via the trajectories collected in previous iterations. Then, the empirical covariance matrices and
the weights w^\widehat{w} are used to approximate Bellman update and construct an optimistic action
value function (line 7). Learn optimistic policy: Next, in the forward inner iteration, the agent
learns a greedy optimistic policy and uses it to explore and collect a new trajectory. After the
outer iteration completes, the greedy policies are output by Algorithm 3.

Algorithm 3 differs from traditional UCB type of algorithms under linear MDP (i.e., LSVI-UCB in Jin
et al., (2020)) as follows. First, Jin et al., (2020) assumes the true representation ϕ∗\phi^{*} is
known with a small model misspecification error, whereas our representation is obtained from
upstream, and hence the upstream learning error affects the learning accuracy here. Second,
Algorithm 3 is applicable to reward functions from a general function class ℛ\mathcal{R}, which do
not need to be linear with respect to the representation. Both of the above differences will
require additional techniques in our analysis of the performance guarantee to be presented in
Section 5.5.

5.5 Sample complexity for downstream online RL

To analyze our downstream online Algorithm 3, we need to characterize how the misspecification
error of the learned representation from upstream affects the downstream suboptimality gap. We
further need to handle the general reward rather than the linear reward. Both make our analysis
different from that in Jin et al., (2020) which directly assumes an approximation of the
representation and works with linear reward functions. We next provide our main result of
downstream online RL in the following theorem and defer the proof to Appendix D.

Theorem 5.9.

Under 5.1, 5.2, 5.3 and 5.4, fix δ∈(0,1)\delta\in(0,1). If we set λ=1\lambda=1 and βn=O⁡
(d​ιn+n​d​ξdown𝐶𝐿𝑂𝑆𝐸\beta_{n}=O\left(d\sqrt{\iota_{n}}+\sqrt{nd}\xi_{\mathrm{down}}\right.
𝑂𝑃𝐸𝑁+p​log⁡n)\left.+\sqrt{p\log n}\right) where ιn=log⁡(2​p​d​n​H​max⁡{ξdown,1}/δ)\iota_{n}=\log\
left(2pdnH\max\{\xi_{\mathrm{down}},1\}/\delta\right). Let π~\tilde{\pi} be the uniform mixture of
π1,…,πn\pi^{1},\ldots,\pi^{n}. Then with probability at least 1−δ1-\delta, the suboptimality gap of
Algorithm 3 satisfies

 VP(∗,T+1),r∗−VP(∗,T+1),rπ~=O~(Hdξdown+Hd1/2Non−1/2max{d,p}).\displaystyle V_{P^{(*,T+1)},r}^
 {*}-V_{P^{(*,T+1)},r}^{{\tilde{\pi}}}=\widetilde{O}\left({Hd\xi_{\mathrm{down}}}+Hd^{1/2}N_   (16)
 {\mathrm{on}}^{-1/2}\max\{d,\sqrt{p}\}\right).

Furthermore, if the linear combination misspecification error ξ\xi in Assumption 5.4 satisfies O~​
(d/Non)\tilde{O}(\sqrt{d}/\sqrt{N_{\mathrm{on}}}), then with at most O~​(H3​d​K2​T​Non+
(H3​d3​K+H5​K2+H5​d2​K)​T2​Non+H5​K3​T​Nond2)\widetilde{O}\left({H^{3}dK^{2}TN_{\mathrm{on}}}+\left
(H^{3}d^{3}K+H^{5}K^{2}+H^{5}d^{2}K\right)T^{2}N_{\mathrm{on}}+\frac{H^{5}K^{3}TN_{\mathrm{on}}}{d^
{2}}\right) trajectories collected in upstream, ξdown\xi_{\mathrm{down}} reduces to O~​(d/Non)\
tilde{O}(\sqrt{d}/\sqrt{N_{\mathrm{on}}}) and the second term in Equation 16 dominates so that the
the suboptimality gap is bounded as

 VP(∗,T+1),r∗−VP(∗,T+1),rπ~=O~(Hd1/2Non−1/2max{d,p}).\displaystyle V_{P^{(*,T+1)},r}^{*}-V_{P
 ^{(*,T+1)},r}^{{\tilde{\pi}}}=\widetilde{O}\left(Hd^{1/2}N_{\mathrm{on}}^{-1/2}\max\{d,\sqrt  (17)
 {p}\}\right).

We first explain the two terms in the suboptimality gap in Equation 16 as follows. The first term
captures how the approximation error of using the estimated representation from upstream affects
the suboptimality gap, and becomes small if such an approximation error is well controlled. The
second term arises from the estimation error of w^h\widehat{w}_{h} in constructing the optimistic
action value function and vanishes as the number NonN_{\mathrm{on}} of samples becomes large. The
dependence on dd and p\sqrt{p} arises from the model complexity of the linear function
approximation class when we seek a concentration of the estimated value function. Thus, if the
learned representation in upstream approximates the ground truth well and ξdown\xi_{\mathrm{down}}
reduces to O~​(d/Non)\tilde{O}(\sqrt{d}/\sqrt{N_{\mathrm{on}}}), then the suboptimality gap is
dominated by the second term O~(Hd1/2Non−1/2max{d,p})\widetilde{O}\left(Hd^{1/2}N_{\mathrm{on}}^{-1
/2}\max\{d,\sqrt{p}\}\right) as in Equation 17. This matches the suboptimality gap O~​(H2​d3/Non)\
widetilde{O}\left(\sqrt{H^{2}d^{3}/N_{\mathrm{on}}}\right)^4^4 4 We rescale the result in Jin
et al., (2020) under the condition of ∑h=1Hrh≤1\sum_{h=1}^{H}r_{h}\leq 1 and transform the regret
bound to the suboptimality gap for a fair comparison. in linear MDP (Jin et al.,, 2020) if we
specialize our reward to be linear and let p=O⁡(d)p=O(d).

Theorem 5.9 also demonstrates that our downstream online RL benefits from the learned
representation from upstream. Compared to the state-of-the-art work under the low-rank MDP with
unknown representation (Uehara et al.,, 2021) which has the suboptimality gap being bounded as O~​
(H5​d4​K2/Non)\widetilde{O}(\sqrt{H^{5}d^{4}K^{2}/N_{\mathrm{on}}})^5^5 5 We convert the results in
discounted setting in (Uehara et al.,, 2021) to our episodic MDP setting by replacing 1/(1−γ)=Θ⁡(H)
1/(1-\gamma)=\Theta(H) and transforming their PAC bound into suboptimality gap., our downstream
online RL has an improved suboptimality gap in terms of the dependence on H,dH,d and KK benefited
from the upstream representation learning.

6 Conclusion

In this paper, we showed that a shared representation of low rank MDPs can be learned more
efficiently and in a transferred manner. We provided upper bounds on the sample complexity of
upstream reward-free multitask RL, and on the suboptimality gap of both downstream offline and
online RL settings with the help of the learned representation from upstream. Our results show that
multitask representation learning is provably more sample efficient than learning each task
individually and capture the benefit of employing the learned representation from upstream to learn
a near-optimal policy of a new task in downstream that shares the same representation. As future
work, we note that our analysis of the downstream RL depends on some assumptions that connect the
upstream and downstream MDPs. It is interesting to explore whether these assumptions can be further
relaxed.

For consistency of notation, in some generic cases, we write sh∼(P,π)s_{h}\sim(P,\pi) for all hh
for the entire episode. But for such cases, s1s_{1} should still be understood as a fixed state and
independent of transition model PP and policy π\pi. For example in ζ1(n,t)\zeta_{1}^{(n,t)} as
defined above, the expectation is taken with respect to a1∼𝒰⁡(𝒜)a_{1}\sim\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{A})
and fixed state s1s_{1}. Furthermore, 𝒰⁡(πt1,…,πtn−1)\mathcal{U}(\pi^{1}_{t},...,\pi^{n-1}_{t})
denotes uniform mixture of previous n−1n-1 exploration policies πt1,…,πtn−1\pi^{1}_{t},...,\pi^
{n-1}_{t}.

Proof Overview: The proof of Theorem 4.2 consists of three main steps, a final sample complexity
step, and several supporting lemmas. Step 1: We develop a new upper bound on model estimation error
for each task, which captures the advantage of joint MLE model estimation over single-task
learning, as shown in Proposition A.1. Step 2: We establish the PCV as an uncertainty measure that
captures the difference between the estimated and ground truth models. This justifies using such a
PCV as a guidance for further exploration, as shown in Proposition A.3. Step 3: We show that the
summation of the PCVs over all iterations is sublinear with respect to the total number NuN_{u} of
iterations, as shown in Proposition A.4, which further implies polynomial efficiency of REFUEL in
learning the models. Complexity characterization: Combining the three steps together with a
contradiction argument yields the final sample complexity.

We provide details for the three main steps in Section A.1-Section A.3, the complexity
characterization in Section A.4, and the supporting lemmas in Section A.5.

A.1 Step 1: A new upper bound on model estimation error

We develop a new upper bound on model estimation error for each task, which captures the advantage
of joint MLE model estimation over single-task learning, as shown in Proposition A.1.

Proposition A.1.

For any n∈[Nu]n\in[N_{u}], task tt, policy πt\pi_{t} and reward rtr^{t}, for all h≥2h\geq 2, we
have

 𝔼sh∼(P^(n,t),πt)ah∼πt\

The following corollary is a direct application of Proposition A.1.

  |VP(∗,t),rtπt−VP^(n,t),rtπt|\displaystyle\left|V_{P^{(*,t)},r^{t}}^{\pi_{t}}-V_{\widehat{P}

  {(n,t)},\pi_{t})\atop a_{h}\sim\pi_{t}}\left[\min\left\{\alpha_{h}^{(n,t)}\left\|\widehat{\  (20)
  phi}_{h}^{(n)}(s_{h},a_{h})\right\|_{(U^{(n,t)}_{h,\widehat{\phi}^{(n,t)}})^{-1}},1\right\}

A.2 Step 2: PCV as a new uncertainty metric

In Proposition A.1 and Corollary A.2, we provide upper bounds on both the total variation distance
between the learned model and the true model, and the difference of value functions under any
policy with arbitrary rewards. For each single task, such upper bounds measure the uncertainty of
model estimation and guide exploration in the next iteration. In multitask RL, although these upper
bounds also hold for each individual source task under any policy and reward even with tighter
terms αh(n,t)\alpha_{h}^{(n,t)}, the RHS of Equation 20 cannot be used to guide exploration for
each task tt individually as in single-task RL because ζh(n,t)\zeta_{h}^{(n,t)} in αh(n,t)\alpha_
{h}^{(n,t)} as a joint MLE Guarantee is unknown individually for each task tt.

This motivate us to jointly consider all the explorations of all source tasks. As we establish
below in Proposition A.3 that our defined new notion of PCV used in Algorithm 1 is a known upper
bound for both the summation of the difference of value function and the summation of total
variation distance over TT tasks, and can therefore serve as an uncertainty quantifier to guide
exploration.

Proposition A.3 (PCV as uncertainty metric).

Given any δ∈(0,|Ψ|−min⁡{T,Kd2})\delta\in\left(0,|\Psi|^{-\min\{T,\frac{K}{d^{2}}\}}\right), for any
nn, policy πt\pi_{t} and reward function rtr^{t} of task t∈[T]t\in[T], set λn=O⁡(d​log⁡(|Φ|​n​T​H/
δ))\lambda_{n}=O(d\log(|\Phi|nTH/\delta)). Then with probability 1−δ1-\delta, we have

  ∑t=1T|VP(∗,t),rtπt−VP^(n,t),rtπt|≤P​C​V​(P^(n,t),b^h(n,t),πt,T)+K​T​ζn,\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^
  {T}\left|V_{P^{(*,t)},r^{t}}^{\pi_{t}}-V_{\widehat{P}^{(n,t)},r^{t}}^{\pi_{t}}\right|{\leq}PCV\
  left(\widehat{P}^{(n,t)},\widehat{b}_{h}^{(n,t)},\pi_{t};T\right)+\sqrt{KT\zeta_{n}},
  ∑t=1T∑h=1H𝔼sh∼(P^(n,t),πt)ah∼πt[fh(n,t)​(sh,ah)]≤P​C​V​(P^(n,t),b^h(n,t),πt,T)+K​T​ζn.\
  displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{h=1}^{H}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{s_{h}\sim(\widehat{P}^{(n,t)},\pi_
  {t})\atop a_{h}\sim\pi_{t}}\left[{f_{h}^{(n,t)}(s_{h},a_{h})}\right]\leq PCV\left(\widehat{P}^
  {(n,t)},\widehat{b}_{h}^{(n,t)},\pi_{t};T\right)+\sqrt{KT\zeta_{n}}.

Proof.

Following from the fact that ∑t=1Tζh(n,t)≤ζn\sum_{t=1}^{T}\zeta_{h}^{(n,t)}\leq\zeta_{n}, for all
hh (see Lemma A.7), and the definitions of αh(n,t)\alpha_{h}^{(n,t)} and αn\alpha_{n} for h≥2h\geq
2, with probability at least 1−δ/21-\delta/2, we have

 ∑t=1T(αh(n,t))2≤n​K​∑t=1T(ζh(n,t)+ζh−1(n,t))+λn​T​d≤2​n​K​ζn+λn​T​d=αn2.\displaystyle\sum_{t
 =1}^{T}(\alpha_{h}^{(n,t)})^{2}\leq nK\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(\zeta_{h}^{(n,t)}+\zeta_{h-1}^      (21)
 {(n,t)}\right)+\lambda_{n}Td\leq 2nK\zeta_{n}+\lambda_{n}Td=\alpha_{n}^{2}.

Moreover, the ratio between αn\alpha_{n} and αh(n,t)\alpha_{h}^{(n,t)} is upper bounded as follows:

 αnαh(n,t)\displaystyle\    =2​n​K​ζn+λn​T​dn​K​ζh(n,t)+n​K​ζh−1(n,t)+λn​d\displaystyle=\sqrt
 frac{\alpha_{n}}{\alpha_   {\frac{2nK\zeta_{n}+\lambda_{n}Td}{nK\zeta_{h}^{(n,t)}+nK\zeta_
 {h}^{(n,t)}}               {h-1}^{(n,t)}+\lambda_{n}d}}
                            ≤2​n​K​ζn+λn​T​dλn​d\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{2nK\zeta_{n}+\
                            lambda_{n}Td}{\lambda_{n}d}}
                            =T+2​K​ln⁡(n​|Φ|​|Ψ|T/δ)d2​ln⁡(n​|Φ|​T/δ)\displaystyle=\sqrt{T+\
                            frac{2K\ln(n|\Phi||\Psi|^{T}/\delta)}{d^{2}\ln(n|\Phi|T/\delta)}}
                            =T+2​Kd2+2​K​T​ln⁡(|Ψ|)d2​ln⁡(n​|Φ|​T/δ)=Bn≤B,\displaystyle=\sqrt
                            {T+\frac{2K}{d^{2}}+\frac{2KT\ln(|\Psi|)}{d^{2}\ln(n|\Phi|T/\      (22)
                            delta)}}=B_{n}\leq B,

where the last inequality follows because Bn≤B=2​T+K/d2B_{n}\leq B=2\sqrt{T+K/d^{2}} if δ∈(0,|Ψ|
−min⁡{T,K}d2)\delta\in{(0,|\Psi|^{-\frac{\min\{T,K\}}{d^{2}}})}.

Following from Corollary A.2, we take the summation over all source tasks and obtain

  ∑t=1T|VP(∗,t),rtπt−VP^(n,t),rtπt|\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left|V_{P^{(*,t)},r^{t}}^{\pi_{t}}

  −1,1}]+∑t=1TK​ζ1(n,t)\displaystyle\scriptstyle\leq\sum_{h=2}^{H}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathop{\mathbb
  {E}}_{s_{h-1}\sim(\widehat{P}^{(n,t)},\pi_{t})\atop a_{h-1}\sim\pi_{t}}\left[\min\left\{\alpha_

  {\phi}^{(n,t)}})^{-1}},1\right\}\right]+\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sqrt{K\zeta_{1}^{(n,t)}}
  =∑h=2H∑t=1Tαh(n,t)​𝔼sh−1∼(P^(n,t),πt)ah−1∼πt[min⁡{‖ϕ^h−1(n)​(sh−1,ah−1)‖(Uh−1,ϕ^(n,t)(n,t))−1,
  (αh(n,t))−1}]+∑t=1TK​ζ1(n,t)\displaystyle\scriptstyle=\sum_{h=2}^{H}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\
  hskip-1.4457pt\alpha_{h}^{(n,t)}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{s_{h-1}\sim(\widehat{P}^{(n,t)},\pi_{t})\

 displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{h=1}^{H}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{s_{h}\sim(\widehat{P}^{(n,t)},\pi_

A.3 Step 3: Sublinear accumulation of PCV

Recall that the exploration policy is derived by an oracle:

 π1n,…,πTn=arg⁡maxπ1,…,πT⁡P​C​V​(P^(n,t),b^h(n,t),πt,T)\displaystyle\pi_{1}^{n},\dots,\pi_{T}
 ^{n}=\mathop{\arg\max}_{\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{T}}PCV\left(\widehat{P}^{(n,t)},\widehat{b}_{h}^   (23)
 {(n,t)},\pi_{t};T\right)

In this step, we show that the summation of exploration-driven reward function PCV over n,t,hn,t,h
is sublinear with respect to the total number NuN_{u} of iterations, as given in Proposition A.4,
which further implies polynomial efficiency of REFUEL in learning the models.

Proposition A.4.

Given any δ∈(0,|Ψ|−min⁡{T,Kd2})\delta\in\left(0,|\Psi|^{-\min\{T,\frac{K}{d^{2}}\}}\right), set λn=
O⁡(d​log⁡(|Φ|​n​T​H/δ))\lambda_{n}=O(d\log(|\Phi|nTH/\delta)). Then with probability 1−δ1-\delta,
under exploration policy {πtn}n∈[Nu]\{\pi_{t}^{n}\}_{n\in[N_{u}]} for each task tt, the summation
of PCVs over nn is sublinear with respect to the number NuN_{u} of iteration rounds:

  ∑n=1Nu{P​C​V​(P^(n,t),b^h(n,t),πtn,T)+K​T​ζn}\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{N_{u}}\left\{PCV\left
  (\widehat{P}^{(n,t)},\widehat{b}_{h}^{(n,t)},\pi_{t}^{n};T\right)+\sqrt{KT\zeta_{n}}\right
  \}
  ≤4​β2β1​H​Nu​T​d​(K2​d​log2⁡(|Φ|​|Ψ|T​Nu​H/δ)+K​d3​T​log2⁡(2​Nu​T​H​|Φ|/δ)+d2​B2​log2⁡
  (Nu​T​H​|Φ|/δ))\displaystyle\quad\scriptstyle\leq\frac{4\beta_{2}}{\beta_{1}}H\sqrt{{N_{u}}
  Td}\left(\sqrt{K^{2}d\log^{2}\left(|\Phi||\Psi|^{T}N_{u}H/\delta\right)}+\sqrt{Kd^{3}T\log^
  {2}(2N_{u}TH|\Phi|/\delta)}+\sqrt{d^{2}B^{2}\log^{2}(N_{u}TH|\Phi|/\delta)}\right)
  +6​B​H2​Nu​T​K​d​(K​log2⁡(|Φ|​|Ψ|T​Nu​H/δ)+d2​log2⁡(Nu​T​H​|Φ|/δ)).\displaystyle\quad+6BH^
  {2}\sqrt{{N_{u}}TKd}\left(\sqrt{K{\log^{2}\left(|\Phi||\Psi|^{T}N_{u}H/\delta\right)}}+\     (24)
  sqrt{d^{2}\log^{2}(N_{u}TH|\Phi|/\delta)}\right).

Proof.

We proceed the bound as follows:

  ∑n=1Nu{P​C​V​(P^(n,t),b^h(n,t),πtn,T)+K​T​ζn}\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{N_{u}}\left\{PCV\left
  (\widehat{P}^{(n,t)},\widehat{b}_{h}^{(n,t)},\pi_{t}^{n};T\right)+\sqrt{KT\zeta_{n}}\right
  \}
  ≤Nu​K​T​ζn+∑n=1Nu∑h=1H−1∑t=1T{𝔼sh∼(P^(n,t),πtn)ah∼πtn[b^h(n,t)​(sh,ah)]}2\displaystyle\quad

A.5 Supporting Lemmas

Recall Uh,ϕ(n,t)=n​𝔼sh∼(P∗,Πtn),ah∼𝒰⁡(𝒜)​[ϕ⁡(sh,ah)​(ϕ⁡(sh,ah))⊤]+λn​IU_{h,\phi}^{(n,t)}=n\mathbb
{E}_{s_{h}\sim(P^{*},\Pi_{t}^{n}),a_{h}\sim\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{A})}\left[\phi(s_{h},a_{h})(\phi(s_
{h},a_{h}))^{\top}\right]+\lambda_{n}I. Then Uh,ϕ^(n)(n,t)U_{h,\widehat{\phi}^{(n)}}^{(n,t)} is the
counterpart of U^h(n,t)\widehat{U}_{h}^{(n,t)} in expectation. The following lemma provides the
concentration of the bonus term. See Lemma 39 in Zanette et al., 2020a () for the version of fixed
ϕ\phi and Lemma 11 in Uehara et al., (2021).

Lemma A.5.

(Concentration of the bonus term). Fix δ∈(0,1)\delta\in(0,1), and set λn=Θ⁡(d​log⁡(2​n​T​H​|Φ|/δ))\
lambda_{n}=\Theta(d\log(2nTH|\Phi|/\delta)) for any nn. With probability at least 1−δ/21-\delta/2,
we have that ∀n∈ℕ+,h∈[H],t∈[T],ϕ^∈Φ,\forall n\in\mathbb{N}^{+},h\in[H],t\in[T],\widehat{\phi}\in\
Phi,

 β1​‖ϕ^h(n)​(s,a)‖(Uh,ϕ^(n,t))−1≤‖ϕ^h(n)​(s,a)‖(U^h(n,t))−1≤β2​‖ϕ^h(n)​(s,a)‖(Uh,ϕ^(n,t))−1.\beta_
 {1}\left\|\widehat{\phi}_{h}^{(n)}(s,a)\right\|_{(U_{h,\widehat{\phi}}^{(n,t)})^{-1}}\leq\left\|\
 widehat{\phi}_{h}^{(n)}(s,a)\right\|_{(\widehat{U}_{h}^{(n,t)})^{-1}}\leq\beta_{2}\left\|\widehat
 {\phi}_{h}^{(n)}(s,a)\right\|_{(U_{h,\widehat{\phi}}^{(n,t)})^{-1}}.

Since b^h(n,t)​(sh,ah)=min⁡{α~n​‖ϕ^h(n)​(s,a)‖(U^h(n,t))−1,B}\widehat{b}_{h}^{(n,t)}(s_{h},a_{h})=\
min\left\{{{\widetilde{\alpha}}}_{n}\left\|\widehat{\phi}_{h}^{(n)}(s,a)\right\|_{(\widehat{U}_{h}^
{(n,t)})^{-1}},B\right\}. Setting α~n=αnβ1{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{n}=\frac{\alpha_{n}}{{\beta_{1}}}
and applying Lemma A.5, we can immediately obtain the following corollary.

Corollary A.6.

Fix δ∈(0,1)\delta\in(0,1), under the same setting of Lemma A.5, with probability at least 1−δ/21-\
delta/2, we have that ∀n∈ℕ+,h∈[H],ϕ∈Φ,\forall n\in\mathbb{N}^{+},h\in[H],\phi\in\Phi,

 phi}}^{(n,t)})^{-1}},B\right\}\leq\widehat{b}_{h}^{(n,t)}(s_{h},a_{h})\leq\frac{\beta_{2}}{\beta_
 {1}}\alpha_{n}\left\|\widehat{\phi}_{h}^{(n)}(s_{h},a_{h})\right\|_{(U_{h,\widehat{\phi}}^
 {(n,t)})^{-1}}.

Recall that fh(n,t)(s,a)=∥P^h(n,t)(⋅|s,a)−Ph(∗,t)(⋅|s,a)∥T​Vf_{h}^{(n,t)}(s,a)=\|\widehat{P}_{h}^
{(n,t)}(\cdot|s,a)-P^{(*,t)}_{h}(\cdot|s,a)\|_{TV} represents the estimation error of task tt in
terms of the total variation distance in the nn-th iteration at step hh, given state ss and action
aa in Algorithm 1. Inspired by the proof of Theorem 21 in Agarwal et al., (2020), We show that if
we uniformly choose the exploration policies for each task, the summation of the estimation error
can be bounded with high probability.

Proof of Lemma A.7.

Consider a sequential conditional probability estimation setting with an instance space 𝒳\mathcal
{X} and a target space 𝒴\mathcal{Y} where the conditional density is given by p⁡(y|x)=f∗​(x,y)p(y|
x)=f^{*}(x,y). We are given a dataset D:={(xi,yi)}i=1nD:=\{(x_{i},y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n} , where xi∼𝒟i=
𝒟i(x1:i−1,y1:i−1)x_{i}\sim\mathcal{D}_{i}=\mathcal{D}_{i}(x_{1:i-1},y_{1:i-1}) and yi∼p(⋅|xi)y_{i}\
sim p(\cdot|x_{i}). Let D′D^{\prime} denote a tangent sequence {(xi′,yi′)}i=1n\{(x_{i}^{\prime},y_
{i}^{\prime})\}_{i=1}^{n} where xi′∼𝒟i(x1:i−1,y1:i−1)x_{i}^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{D}_{i}(x_{1:i-1},y_
{1:i-1}) and yi′∼p(⋅|xi′)y_{i}^{\prime}\sim p(\cdot|x_{i}^{\prime}). Further, we consider a
function class ℱ:(𝒳×𝒴)→R\mathcal{F}:(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y})\rightarrow R and assume that the
reachability condition f∗∈ℱf^{*}\in\mathcal{F} holds.

We first introduce two useful lemmas from Agarwal et al., (2020).

Lemma A.8 (Lemma 25 of Agarwal et al., (2020)).

For any two conditional probability densities f1,f2f_{1},f_{2} and any distribution 𝒟∈△⁡(𝒳)\mathcal
{D}\in\triangle(\mathcal{X}), we have

 𝔼x∼D∥f1(x,⋅)−f2(x,⋅)∥T​V2≤−2log𝔼x∼𝒟,y∼f2(⋅|x)[exp(−12log(f2(x,y)/f1(x,y)))].\displaystyle\mathbb
 {E}_{x\sim D}\|f_{1}(x,\cdot)-f_{2}(x,\cdot)\|^{2}_{TV}\leq-2\log\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathcal{D},y\
 sim f_{2}(\cdot|x)}\left[\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\log(f_{2}(x,y)/f_{1}(x,y))\right)\right].

Lemma A.9 (Lemma 24 of Agarwal et al., (2020)).

Let DD ba a dataset of nn samples and D′D^{\prime} be corresponding tangent sequence. Let L⁡(f,D)=
∑i=1nl⁡(f,(xi,yi))L(f,D)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}l(f,(x_{i},y_{i})) be any function that decomposes
additively across examples where ll is any function, and let f^​(D)\widehat{f}(D) be any estimator
taking as input random variable DD and with range ℱ\mathcal{F}. Then

We next introduce a one-step back lemma, which extends the one-step back inequality for
infinite-horizon stationary MDP in Uehara et al., (2021); Agarwal et al., (2020) to non-stationary
transition kernels with finite horizon. The lemma shows that for any function g∈𝒮×𝒜→ℝg\in\mathcal
{S}\times\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}, policy π\pi and transition kernel PP, we can upper bound
the expectation 𝔼sh∼(P,π)ah∼π[g⁡(sh,ah)]\scriptstyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{s_{h}\sim(P,{\pi})\atop a_
{h}\sim\pi}[g(s_{h},a_{h})] by the product of two terms. The first term represents the convergence
guarantee of g⁡(sh,ah)g(s_{h},a_{h}) following other policies, which is 𝔼sh∼(P∗,Π)ah∼𝒰⁡(𝒜)[g2​
(sh,ah)]\scriptstyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{s_{h}\sim(P^{*},\Pi)\atop a_{h}\sim\mathcal{U}(\mathcal
{A})}[g^{2}(s_{h},a_{h})]. The second term can be described as the distribution shift coefficient
𝔼sh−1∼(P,π)ah−1∼π[‖ϕh−1​(sh−1,ah−1)‖(Uh−1,ϕ)−1]\scriptstyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{s_{h-1}\sim(P,\pi)\
atop a_{h-1}\sim\pi}\left[\left\|\phi_{h-1}(s_{h-1},a_{h-1})\right\|_{(U_{h-1,\phi})^{-1}}\right],
which measures the difference caused by distribution shift from π\pi and other policies.

Appendix B Proof of Lemma 5.6

Lemma 5.6 serves a central role for bridging the upstream and downstream learning, which shows that
the feature ϕ^\widehat{\phi} learned in upstream is a ξd​o​w​n\xi_{down}-approximate feature map
and can approximate the true feature in the new task.

∎

Appendix C Proof of Theorem 5.8

Recall ξdown=ξ+CL​CR​T​v​ϵuκu\xi_{\mathrm{down}}=\xi+\frac{C_{L}C_{R}Tv\epsilon_{u}}{\kappa_{u}}
and for any h∈[H]h\in[H], we define

  Ph(∗,T+1)(⋅|s,a)=⟨ϕh∗(s,a),μh(∗,T+1)(⋅)⟩,\displaystyle P_{h}^{(*,T+1)}(\cdot|s,a)=\langle\phi_
  {h}^{*}(s,a),\mu_{h}^{(*,T+1)}(\cdot)\rangle,
  P¯h(⋅|s,a)=⟨ϕ^h(s,a),μ^h∗(⋅)⟩.\displaystyle\overline{P}_{h}(\cdot|s,a)=\langle\widehat{\phi}_{h}
  (s,a),\widehat{\mu}^{*}_{h}(\cdot)\rangle.

Given a reward function rr, for any function f:𝒮↦ℝf:\mathcal{S}\mapsto\mathbb{R} and h∈[H]h\in[H],
we define the transition operators and their corresponding Bellman operators as

  (Ph(∗,T+1)​f)​(s,a)=∫s′⟨ϕh∗​(s,a),μh(∗,T+1)​(s′)⟩​f​(s′)​d​s′,\displaystyle(P_{h}^{(*,T+1)}\
  hskip-1.4457ptf)(s,a)\hskip-1.4457pt=\hskip-3.25201pt\int_{s^{\prime}}\hskip-1.4457pt\langle\
  phi_{h}^{*}(s,a),\mu_{h}^{(*,T+1)}\hskip-1.4457pt(s^{\prime})\rangle f(s^{\prime})ds^{\prime}\
  hskip-2.168pt,
  (𝔹h​f)​(s,a)=rh​(s,a)+(Ph(∗,T+1)​f)​(s,a),\displaystyle(\mathbb{B}_{h}f)(s,a)\hskip-0.72229pt=\
  hskip-1.4457ptr_{h}(s,a)\hskip-1.4457pt+\hskip-1.4457pt(P_{h}^{(*,T+1)}\hskip-1.4457ptf)(s,a),
  (P¯h​f)​(s,a)=∫s′⟨ϕ^h​(s,a),μ^h∗​(s′)​f​(s′)⟩​d​s′,\displaystyle(\overline{P}_{h}f)(s,a)=\int_{s
  ^{\prime}}\langle\widehat{\phi}_{h}(s,a),\widehat{\mu}_{h}^{*}(s^{\prime})f(s^{\prime})\rangle
  ds^{\prime},
  (𝔹¯h​f)​(s,a)=rh​(s,a)+(P¯h​f)​(s,a).\displaystyle(\overline{\mathbb{B}}_{h}f)(s,a)=r_{h}(s,a)+
  (\overline{P}_{h}f)(s,a).

We further denote (𝔹^h​V^h+1)​(s,a)=rh​(s,a)+ϕ^h​(s,a)⊤​w^h(\widehat{\mathbb{B}}_{h}\widehat{V}_
{h+1})(s,a)=r_{h}(s,a)+\widehat{\phi}_{h}(s,a)^{\top}\widehat{w}_{h}, h∈[H]h\in[H].

We remark that throughout this section, the expectation is taken with respect to the transition
kernel of the target task, i.e., P(∗,T+1)P^{(*,T+1)}.

Proof Overview: The proof of Theorem 5.8 consists of two main steps and a final suboptimality gap
characterization. Step 1: We decompose the suboptimality gap into the summation of the uncertainty
metric of each step in Lemma C.2. We note that the reward function rhr_{h} here is from a general
class, not necessarily a linear function. Step 2: We provide an upper bound on the Bellman update
error as shown in Lemma C.4, where our main technical contribution lies in capturing the impact of
the misspecification of the representation taken from upstream estimation on such error. 
Suboptimality gap characterization: Based on the first two steps, we select uncertainty metric Γh\
Gamma_{h} and obtain an instance-dependent suboptimality gap, which we further bound under the
feature coverage assumption.

We provide details for the two main steps and the suboptimality gap characterization in Section C.1
-Section C.3, respectively.

C.1 Suboptimality decomposition

In this step, we decompose the suboptimality gap into the summation of the uncertainty metric of
each step in Lemma C.2. We note that the reward function rr here is from a general class, not
necessarily a linear function. To this end, we first provide the following lemma.

Lemma C.1.

If |(𝔹h​V^h+1−𝔹^h​V^h+1)​(s,a)|≤Γh​(s,a)|(\mathbb{B}_{h}\widehat{V}_{h+1}-\widehat{\mathbb{B}}_{h}\
widehat{V}_{h+1})(s,a)|\leq\Gamma_{h}(s,a) for all (h,s,a)∈[H]×𝒮×𝒜(h,s,a)\in[H]\times\mathcal{S}\
times\mathcal{A}, then it holds that (𝔹h​V^h+1)​(s,a)≤1(\mathbb{B}_{h}\widehat{V}_{h+1})(s,a)\leq
1, ∀(h,s,a)∈[H]×𝒮×𝒜\forall(h,s,a)\in[H]\times\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}.

Proof.

 {V}_{h+1})(s,a)\leq\max_{a_{h+1},\ldots,a_{H}}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{h^{\prime}=h}^{H}r_{h^{\
 prime}}(s_{h^{\prime}},a_{h^{\prime}})\middle|s_{h}=s,a_{h}=a\right].

We prove it by induction. For h′=Hh^{\prime}=H, since V^H+1=0\widehat{V}_{H+1}=0, we have

that ∑h=1Hrh≤1\sum_{h=1}^{H}r_{h}\leq 1.

Further note that

                     sum_{h^{\prime}=h+1}^{H}r_{h^{\prime}}(s_{h^{\prime}},a_{h^{\prime}})\middle|
                     s_{h+1}=s,a_{h+1}=a\right],

We denote the Bellman update error as ζh​(s,a)=(𝔹h​V^h+1)​(s,a)−Q^h​(s,a)\zeta_{h}(s,a)=(\mathbb{B}
_{h}\widehat{V}_{h+1})(s,a)-\widehat{Q}_{h}(s,a). The following lemma shows that it suffices to

Lemma C.2.

Suppose with probability at least 1−δ1-\delta, for all (h,s,a)∈[H]×𝒮×𝒜(h,s,a)\in[H]\times\mathcal

≤2​Γh​(s,a)0\leq\zeta_{h}(s,a)\leq 2\Gamma_{h}(s,a). Moreover, it holds that for any policy π\pi,

 {h},a_{h})|s_{1}=s].

{h}\leq r_{h}(s,a)+\widehat{\phi}_{h}(s,a)^{\top}\widehat{w}_{h}-\Gamma_{h}(s,a)=(\widehat{\mathbb

C.2 Bounding Bellman update error |(𝔹h​V^h+1−𝔹^h​V^h+1)​(s,a)||(\mathbb{B}_{h}\widehat{V}_{h+1}-\
widehat{\mathbb{B}}_{h}\widehat{V}_{h+1})(s,a)|

In this step, we provide an upper bound on the Bellman update error as shown in Lemma C.4, where
the main effort lies in analyzing the impact of the misspecification of the representation taken
from upstream estimation. To this end, we first introduce a concentration lemma that upper-bounds
the stochastic noise in regression.

Lemma C.3.

Under the setting of Theorem 5.8, if we choose λ=1\lambda=1, β⁡(δ)=cβ​(d​ι⁡(δ)
+d​Noff​ξdown+p​log⁡Noff)\beta(\delta)=c_{\beta}\left(d\sqrt{\iota(\delta)}+\sqrt{dN_{\mathrm
{off}}}\xi_{\mathrm{down}}+\sqrt{p\log{N_{\mathrm{off}}}}\right) where ι⁡(δ)=log⁡(2​p​d​H​Noff​max⁡
{ξdown,1}/δ)\iota(\delta)=\log\left(2pdHN_{\mathrm{off}}\max\{\xi_{\mathrm{down}},1\}/\delta\
right), there exists an absolute constant C~\widetilde{C} such that with probability at least 1−δ1-
\delta, it holds that for all h∈[H]h\in[H],

  ‖∑τ=1Noffϕ^h​(shτ,ahτ)​[(Ph(∗,T+1)​V^h+1)​(shτ,ahτ)−V^h+1​(sh+1τ)]‖Λh−1≤C~​[d​ι+p​log⁡Noff].\
  displaystyle\left\lVert\sum_{\tau=1}^{N_{\mathrm{off}}}\widehat{\phi}_{h}(s_{h}^{\tau},a_{h}^{\
  tau})\left[(P_{h}^{(*,T+1)}\widehat{V}_{h+1})(s_{h}^{\tau},a_{h}^{\tau})-\widehat{V}_{h+1}(s_
  {h+1}^{\tau})\right]\right\rVert_{\Lambda_{h}^{-1}}\leq\widetilde{C}\left[d\sqrt{\iota}+\sqrt{p\
  log{N_{\mathrm{off}}}}\right].

Proof.

Note that our reward functions here are selected from a general function class ℛ\mathcal{R}, not
necessarily linear with respect to the feature function ϕ^\widehat{\phi}. The value function V^h+1\
widehat{V}_{h+1} has the form of

 V⁡(⋅):=min⁡{maxa∈𝒜⁡wT​ϕ​(⋅,a)+r⁡(⋅,a)+β​ϕ​(⋅,a)⊤​Λ−1​ϕ​(⋅,a),1}\displaystyle V(\cdot):=\min\
 left\{\max_{a\in\mathcal{A}}w^{T}\phi(\cdot,a)+r(\cdot,a)+\beta\sqrt{\phi(\cdot,a)^{\top}\    (47)
 Lambda^{-1}\phi(\cdot,a)},1\right\}

for some w∈ℝdw\in\mathbb{R}^{d}, r∈ℛr\in\mathcal{R} and positive definite matrix Λ⪰λ​Id\Lambda\
succeq\lambda I_{d}. Let 𝒱\mathcal{V} be the function class of V⁡(⋅)V(\cdot) and 𝒩ε\mathcal{N}_{\
varepsilon} be the ε\varepsilon-covering number of 𝒱\mathcal{V} with respect to the distance dist⁡
(V,V′)=sups|V⁡(s)−V′​(s)|\mathrm{dist}(V,V^{\prime})=\sup_{s}|V(s)-V^{\prime}(s)|.

Note that for any h∈[H],v∈ℝdh\in[H],v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}, we have

 |v⊤​w^h|\            =|v⊤​Λh−1​∑τ=1Noffϕ^h​(shτ,ahτ)​V^h+1​(sh+1τ)|\displaystyle=\left|v^{\top}\
 displaystyle\left|v^ Lambda_{h}^{-1}\sum_{\tau=1}^{N_{\mathrm{off}}}\widehat{\phi}_{h}(s_{h}^{\
 {\top}\widehat{w}_   tau},a_{h}^{\tau})\widehat{V}_{h+1}(s_{h+1}^{\tau})\right|
 {h}\right|
                      ≤∑τ=1Noff|v⊤​Λh−1​ϕ^h​(shτ,ahτ)|\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\tau=1}^{N_{\mathrm
                      {off}}}\left|v^{\top}\Lambda_{h}^{-1}\widehat{\phi}_{h}(s_{h}^{\tau},a_{h}^
                      {\tau})\right|
                      ≤[∑τ=1Noff‖v‖Λh−12]​[∑τ=1Noff‖ϕ^h​(shτ,ahτ)‖Λh−12]\displaystyle{\leq}\sqrt{\
                      left[\sum_{\tau=1}^{N_{\mathrm{off}}}\left\lVert v\right\rVert^{2}_{\Lambda_
                      {h}^{-1}}\right]\left[\sum_{\tau=1}^{N_{\mathrm{off}}}\left\lVert\widehat{\
                      phi}_{h}(s_{h}^{\tau},a_{h}^{\tau})\right\rVert^{2}_{\Lambda_{h}^{-1}}\
                      right]}
                      ≤‖v‖2​d​Noff/λ,\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{2}\sqrt{dN_{\
                      mathrm{off}}/\lambda},

where the second inequality follows from Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and the last inequality follows
from the fact that ‖v‖Λh−1=‖Λh−1‖op1/2⋅‖v‖2≤1/λ​‖v‖2\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{\Lambda_{h}^{-1}}=\

C.3 Suboptimality gap characterization: proof of Theorem 5.8

Based on the previous lemmas, we establish the suboptimality gap.

In Lemma C.2, let Γh=β​‖ϕ^h​(s,a)‖Λh−1+ξdown\Gamma_{h}=\beta\left\lVert\widehat{\phi}_{h}(s,a)\

as large as

 {2}TN_{\mathrm{off}}}+\left(H^{3}d^{3}K+H^{5}K^{2}+H^{5}d^{2}K\right)T^{2}N_{\mathrm{off}}+\frac
 {H^{5}K^{3}TN_{\mathrm{off}}}{d^{2}}\right),

then ξdown\xi_{\mathrm{down}} reduces to O~​(d/Noff)\tilde{O}(\sqrt{d}/\sqrt{N_{\mathrm{off}}}) by
definition and Theorem 4.2. Hence, the suboptimality gap is dominated by the last term in Equation 
58 and is given by

 O~(Hd1/2Non−1/2max{d,p}).\displaystyle\widetilde{O}\left(Hd^{1/2}N_{\mathrm{on}}^{-1/2}\max\{d,\
 sqrt{p}\}\right).

Appendix D Proof of Theorem 5.9

Recall ξdown=ξ+CL​CR​T​v​ϵuκu\xi_{\mathrm{down}}=\xi+\frac{C_{L}C_{R}Tv\epsilon_{u}}{\kappa_{u}}
and for any h∈[H]h\in[H], we define Ph(∗,T+1)(⋅|s,a)=⟨ϕh∗(s,a),μh(∗,T+1)(⋅)⟩,P¯h(⋅|s,a)=⟨ϕ^h(s,a),μ

  Ph(∗,T+1)​f​(s,a)=∫s′⟨ϕh∗​(s,a),μh(∗,T+1)​(s′)⟩​f​(s′)​d​s′,\displaystyle P_{h}^{(*,T+1)}f(s,a)=
  \int_{s^{\prime}}\langle\phi_{h}^{*}(s,a),\mu_{h}^{(*,T+1)}(s^{\prime})\rangle f(s^{\prime})ds^
  {\prime},
  (P¯h​f)​(s,a)=∫s′ϕ^​(s,a)​μ^∗​(s′)​f​(s′)​d​s′,\displaystyle(\overline{P}_{h}f)(s,a)=\int_{s^{\

  (P^h​f)​(s,a)=∫s′ϕ^​(s,a)​μ^​(s′)​f​(s′)​d​s′.\displaystyle(\widehat{P}_{h}f)(s,a)=\int_{s^{\
